a16z is challenging Silicon Valley’s love for drop-outs by launching a school
2026-09-22

a16z is challenging Silicon Valley’s love for drop-outs by launching a school

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Venture capital firm Andreessen Horowitz is launching a school called The Horowitz Andreessen Academy aimed at aspiring founders who have just graduated high school and have not yet attended college. The academy is aiming to train these high school grads with hands-on coursework on a variety of topics ranging from the technical (such as “AI Research and Models”) to life philosophy (such as “The Geometry of Luck,” which promises to teach students “about arranging a life so good fortune flows through it.”) Students will also work on personal projects. The school’s main pitch is that it promises to provide students with mentors who are big names in tech. The school is currently accepting applications for its first year, which will be a one-year program that charges no tuition, and is open to about 50 students, “chosen on proof of work: what they have built, shipped, or earned,” according to the school’s FAQ. The academy is located in San Francisco and students will be responsible for their own housing (although the school is working to help them find it and match them with other student roommates). One item to note: The academy is currently not an accredited school, so participants will not earn a degree, or college credits that transfer elsewhere. In 2028, The Horowitz Andreessen Academy — which is a for-profit venture that has raised $42 million — hopes to offer a two-year program. At that point, it plans to charge tuition “similar in cost to an elite private university,” the school’s CEO Gagan Biyani said in a post on X. The schools, which is open to high school graduates, even if they are younger than 18, may also expand beyond 50 students in the future, an a16z spokesperson tells TechCrunch. In exchange for that elite-tuition price tag, students will gain access to 200 speakers and mentors, including people from 10 founding corporate partners: Anduril, Anthropic, Coinbase, Google, Meta, Nvidia, OpenAI, Palantir, Replit, and Stripe. Those companies and dozens of others hope to hire from the grads of this school, Biyani said. So, this is like if Y Combinator (the most famed startup school) and Peter Thiel’s Fellowship Program (a famed program for young people who opt to build companies instead of attend college) had a baby. The difference is, instead of a short-term experience ending with funding for a company, these students will pay tuition, earn tech-trade skills, and have an inside track at tech industry jobs. Or they may also have a startup in the works, built with access to a lot of mentorship. As the founders of the academy describe, it’s meant to be a new, middle ground between obtaining a full degree before entering the tech industry and dropping out to start a company. In this era when AI has been hampering the entry-level job market, gaining access to jobs may be enough reason for some young people to try this new trade school-like approach. Trump Administration to Cancel Obamacare Coverage for 760,000 Enrollees Over Alleged Fraud

Trump Administration to Cancel Obamacare Coverage for 760,000 Enrollees Over Alleged Fraud

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Vice President JD Vance speaks during a news conference on fraud at the Eisenhower Executive Office Building on the White House Campus in Washington, D.C., on September 22, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Trump Administration officials on Tuesday announced that they would terminate the Obamacare coverage of 760,000 enrollees, alleging widespread fraud in enrollment. Vice President JD Vance, who leads a White House task force aimed at eliminating fraud, said while speaking to reporters that $2.2 billion in taxpayer funds would be saved by removing the hundreds of thousands of Affordable Care Act (ACA) enrollees from the public insurance marketplaces. “We expect that most of these people are fraudulently enrolled,” Vance said. He added that the Administration would also work to verify the eligibility of roughly 419,000 additional enrollees by ensuring that they are legal residents of the U.S. and meet the income threshold to receive Obamacare coverage. The Administration will also place a six month moratorium on new health insurance brokers and agents, who help individuals sign up for health plans, Vance said. Read More: Trump Pledges to Issue $500 Refunds to Nearly a Million Obamacare Enrollees. Are You Eligible? The Administration’s move to eject fraudulent enrollees from the Obamacare insurance marketplaces is the latest in its broader efforts to crack down on fraud. An online “ledger” maintained by the White House asserts that the Vance-led anti-fraud task force has uncovered nearly $250 billion worth of fraud since Trump returned to office last January, including almost $100 billion at the Department of Health and Human Services. That crackdown also saw the Administration pause $867.5 million in Medicaid payments to California and $199 million in Minnesota in July, an action for which Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. cited “suspected fraud and noncompliance.” Both states have pushed back on the claims. The Administration has additionally targeted alleged fraud in food stamps, childcare facilities, COVID-era relief funding, student loans, and homelessness funding, among other areas. Critics have accused it of disproportionally focusing on Democratic-led states in its crackdown. An analysis from the Congressional Budget Office (CBO) released last August estimated that 2.3 million Obamacare enrollees improperly claimed premium tax credits by intentionally overstating their income in 2025. The Trump Administration has also claimed that agents and brokers have been responsible for illegally enrolling tens of thousands of people in ACA plans. The 760,000 enrollees now being removed from the ACA rolls by the Trump Administration represent about 4% of the estimated 19.2 million people receiving coverage through the marketplaces in 2026. That total has sharply declined from its peak of more than 24 million last year as ACA premiums have surged following the expiration of subsidies that helped reduce costs for millions of enrollees at the end of 2025. A poll conducted by health policy research group KFF in June found that most voters believe there is at least some fraud in government health programs, and that more than 70% feel it is an extremely or very important issue for candidates to discuss ahead of the midterm elections. For Republican voters, fraud topped all health care issues asked about in the survey, including costs. But health care costs, which have become increasingly expensive in the U.S. in recent years, weighed more significantly for voters overall, and the poll found that only a minority of those surveyed believed that reducing fraud in government programs would reduce such costs for them personally. Amid the Trump Administration’s moves to withhold millions in Medicaid payments, 71% also voiced the belief that preserving access to coverage through the insurance program was more important than rooting out fraud. Meanwhile, 65% believed that the Administration’s Medicaid payment deferrals were mostly politically motivated.

Trump’s U.N. Address Offers a Doctrine of Peace Through Force
2026-09-22

Trump’s U.N. Address Offers a Doctrine of Peace Through Force

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings U.S. President Donald Trump speaks during the United Nations General Assembly in New York City on Sept. 22, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In his speech to the United Nations General Assembly on Tuesday, President Donald Trump spun a familiar web of rhetoric regarding U.S. foreign policy—one that highlights force as a method of achieving peace. In a speech that moved from Iran to Venezuela, Cuba, and beyond, Trump presented something approaching a foreign-policy doctrine: American military might is not the alternative to diplomacy but the leverage that makes it possible. Read More: Iran War Has Cost U.S. Nearly $40 Billion and Fueled Inflation, CBO Finds David Satterfield, Director of Rice University's Baker Institute for Public Policy, tells TIME that the speech Tuesday demonstrates a position held by the Trump Administration that “force is the only currency of value.” “The strong do as they will and the weak suffer as they must,” he says, further characterizing the approach. In Trump’s telling, strength is not merely a deterrent. It is something to be used—through military action and economic isolation—to force negotiations or impose an outcome. Trump has repeatedly distilled his foreign policy strategy into three words: “peace through strength.” The examples he gave of this policy in action illustrate how he may see the U.S. functioning as a global enforcer going forward. His definition of the phrase has previously been suggested by its use in Department of Defense ads and in his online endorsements of fellow Republicans. He recently credited this approach for the joint U.S.-Israel strikes that initiated the current conflict with Iran, posting to Truth Social in March: “PEACE THROUGH STRENGTH TO PUT IT MILDLY!!!” Trump’s approach also encompasses economic coercion. In the case of Iran, he said the U.S. had initially offered economic cooperation, turned to military action when Tehran refused, and was now seeking its “complete economic isolation” to push it back toward negotiations. Trump grouped Cuba among those affected by U.S. economic pressure campaigns on Tuesday. He described a government under mounting strain and predicted political change, declaring that Cuba was a “failed” nation but that “freedom will be coming.” Cuban delegates departed in protest. Yet Trump presented such coercion as compatible with his role as a global peacemaker. He claimed credit for resolving eight wars since returning to office, although that claim has been widely disputed. In tandem, these elements suggest that Trump sees taking action as a superior means of enacting change. He proposes the approaches as being almost diametrically opposed: While others only speak of making a difference, he acts. “While others have spoken of peace, I have made peace. While others have ignored threats, I have confronted them,” Trump said in front of the General Assembly. “While others have talked of courage, America has shown it.” Satterfield says that this speech is mostly “old wine rebottled” for Trump. But it clarified how the President is applying his longstanding ideas today: using military action and economic coercion not merely to deter adversaries, but to force negotiations and shape political outcomes. While Trump sees Iran as an example of his “peace through strength” ethos, the conflict is ongoing, and it remains to be seen how it fits into the pattern that he is hoping to establish. Addressing the topic seemed all but unavoidable heading into the General Assembly. In doing so, Trump said that he had a choice ahead of him: Move forward with a peace deal or “annihilate the Islamic Republic.” The comments came amid stalled negotiations in the war that the U.S. and Israel launched in February. “Will a deal be made with Iran that lets them rebuild and create a far greater country than it ever was before, maybe one of the greatest in the Middle East or even the world?” he asked. “Or do I annihilate the Islamic Republic, and do it quickly, never giving them a chance to kill and destroy people and countries again?” Members of Iran’s delegation walked out during Trump’s remarks, an immediate demonstration of the gulf between his coercive message and the adversary that he may hope to persuade by it. In his speech, he also issued threats against cartel leaders, defending his campaign of drone strikes against suspected drug boats in the Caribbean. These strikes have been criticized by international organizations, legal experts, and human rights groups, which state that the military actions violate international law and constitute extrajudicial killings. “The cartels are the ISIS of the Western Hemisphere—not good people,” he said. “Like ISIS, they should be killed, exiled, or detained as enemy combatants without the possibility of release, which is what we’re doing.” But while these messages are largely meant to promote the very strength that Trump relies upon in his endeavors, the potency of his remarks may be dwindling amid contested claims of success. Kristian Coates Ulrichsen, a fellow for the Middle East at the Baker Institute, tells TIME that Trump’s speech seems “consistent with the narrative of American dominance” that he has long used to justify military action. Trump suggested Tuesday that he considers Venezuela a leading example. In January, the U.S. military launched Operation Absolute Resolve—a nighttime raid in Caracas that captured Venezuela’s president Nicolás Maduro and his wife, Cilia Flores. Both were transported to New York City to face federal drug-trafficking indictments. The move paved the way for a transition of power in Venezuela, with former Vice President Delcy Rodríguez ascending to the role of acting President. It also led to an August agreement giving a U.S.-linked venture a controlling stake and long-term development rights in fields containing an estimated 65 billion barrels of oil. Trump said the deal “more than doubles” American oil reserves. “We’re working closely with the leaders of Venezuela to forge a much better future for the Venezuelan people,” Trump said in his speech. “Last month we signed the largest oil deal in history, covering 65 billion barrels of oil, which will benefit both Venezuela and the United States enormously.” Trump also suggested that U.S. actions had established peace and a route to prosperity for Venezuelans. However, with an acting President who is yet to be elected making deals that may take years to reach fruition, and are likely to draw legal challenges in the meantime, the full picture remains to be seen. But there is one thing the Venezuela operation might have helped accomplish, which is to establish a potential pattern of a U.S. foreign policy that is defined by unilateral intervention. Following a large show of force, Trump has been quick to claim victory, even while the impact of U.S. involvement is still playing out for the people whom it directly affects. Another example is the conflict between Israel and Hamas. In his remarks, Trump gave himself credit for ending the war in Gaza, even though key aspects of the peace deal Trump helped broker in October 2025—including Hamas’s disarmament, a long-term governance plan, and the delivery of needed humanitarian aid in Gaza—are largely stalled. Perceived success in Venezuela “arguably triggered the overreach that we saw in the decision to begin military operations against Iran without a Plan B if initial objectives were not achieved,” Ulrichsen says.But the limitations of such rhetoric are most “visible” in the Iran conflict, according to Ulrichsen, who says the U.S. has not been able to “translate rhetoric into gains on the ground.” Trump has repeatedly predicted that the war would end after the November midterm elections, saying last week that it would end “immediately after” them. He endorsed this belief on Tuesday, as well. Trump initially told reporters that U.S. envoys had held a “very good” three-hour meeting with “mediators of Iran." “They had a very good meeting, and we'll see how it turns out. But certainly, it's to their advantage to make a deal, and we can do that,” he said. Negar Mortazavi, a senior nonresident fellow at the Center for International Policy, emphasized the point that concrete outcomes in the Middle East may prove critical for Trump. “President Trump’s ‘peace through strength’ approach has yet to produce the agreement he wants with Iran,” Mortazavi says. “Trump’s argument leaves a central question unanswered: How does further destruction secure an agreement on Washington’s terms when the pressure so far has not?” “For now, Trump can point to the damage U.S. forces have inflicted, but the war’s central political disputes remain unresolved,” she adds. The outcomes of these most recent efforts—and whether they moved the needle on peace efforts—remain undetermined. Subsequently, Trump turned to the same takeaway that he shared in his speech, which is that Iran is facing either “potential greatness or obliteration.” “It does seem as if such rhetoric is producing diminishing results as his tone gradually loses its shock value,” Ulrichsen observes.

Malcolm Gladwell on Why We Need More Cops and Why Assault-Rifle Bans Don't Work

Malcolm Gladwell on Why We Need More Cops and Why Assault-Rifle Bans Don't Work

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Malcolm Gladwell in Hudson, N.Y., on Sept. 9, 2024. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Malcolm Gladwell is one of America’s foremost chroniclers of curious social phenomena and what’s behind them. In The Tipping Point, he examined why things seem to suddenly change. In Blink, he scrutinized the power and limits of snap judgments. In Outliers, he delved into why some people achieve extraordinary success. In his first book set entirely in the U.S. (out Sept. 29), The American Way of Killing, which is based on a 2023 season of his podcast Revisionist History, he probes into America’s continuing struggle with gun violence. On the eve of his 12-stop book tour, Gladwell told TIME that Americans on both sides of the gun-control argument are not being honest—about the police, about the Second Amendment, and about what really causes gun homicides. This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity. You’ve been interested in crime for some time. Why did it take you so long to get to guns? My early books were about the importance of environment and context in shaping our behavior. You don't really get to guns from that perspective. I had to work through a lot of my interests in the environment before I could turn around and say, "Now wait a minute, what about this?” Also, I was much more interested in understanding universal questions about criminality, and this book is really about very America-specific things. You've subtitled it The Invention of an Epidemic. Is gun violence not an epidemic? What I mean is, it's an epidemic we invented. There are certain traits that countries have that are inherent; the Swiss are shaped by their geography, Jamaicans will go on and on about how the fact they have no snakes shapes who they are. I don't think American criminality is about anything inherent to the United States. I think it's a problem that was brought about by a wide set of very specific choices, misapprehensions, and errors. There's no reason why the United States cannot have the same level of homicide as its Western European peers. It just chose a different direction. No. Assault-rifle bans of the sort that have been passed and contemplated in the United States are completely pointless. They're just a charade, and they are a distraction from the real issues. I think gun control can play an important role in violence prevention. But empty theatrical exercises in removing a class of rifles because they look a certain way do not move the needle. Would pistol regulation be more effective? I don't know how practically you would do that. But if I could wave a magic wand and remove all the handguns in the United States, the country would be dramatically safer overnight. There's no question about that. In places where there are vanishingly few handguns, when people get into an argument, they don't shoot each other. At worst, they stab each other, and when you stab someone, you have—what is it, one-sixth?—the chance of killing them as you do if you shoot them. You suggest that Americans have profoundly misunderstood the real history of the Second Amendment. What is it really about? Slavery. The reason the Second Amendment begins with that clause about the militias is that the people who were called upon to ratify the Constitution were worried about their slaves. In many parts of the South, slaves greatly outnumbered their white masters, and as a result, the white masters were terrified of uprisings, of being killed in their beds. To deal with that, they had a whole system of slave patrols, armed militias who went around and kept slaves in check, and they were extremely concerned that this new thing called the United States would limit the ability of Southern plantation owners to run their slave patrols. So they said: because slave patrols are so important to us, you can't take all our guns. That's the Second Amendment, and we have somehow erased that particular history from our consideration of it today. Is this a majority enough view that it would stand up in, say, a very originalist-minded Supreme Court? This history has been known for decades now. The originalists are not nearly as serious in their consideration of history as they claim to be. They're just using it in an extremely selective way to support a predisposition they have towards gun ownership. Are trauma surgeons one of the reasons that Americans don't care more about gun violence? Murder statistics go down year after year. Some of that is a legitimate decrease in violence. Some of it is the fact that a lot of people who would have been dead are now being kept alive by virtue of very skilled doctors. There’s a statistic I use in the book that says that if you held the level of medical care constant at 1960 levels, we'd have 75,000 homicides every year in the United States, and we have 12,000. It's a huge difference. I don't think we would be as blasé about homicides if there were 75,000 of them every year. How much of the gun-violence problem is actually a crime problem? This is where popular understanding and facts diverge. A lot of people think that gun violence is something that is used in the commission of some other criminal act—to defend the drug trade, to rob someone's house. In fact, the overwhelming amount of gun violence is young people arguing with each other and using guns to resolve some grievance or exact revenge. We design our system on the assumption that gun violence is a rational thing that people do to get something. It doesn't work, because that's not what it is. It's kids with beefs, trapped in cycles of revenge and retaliation, who don't have any other means of resolving their conflicts. Somewhat controversially, considering your usual audience, you advocate for a lot more cops. Why is that? Because America doesn't have any police officers. It's not controversial. It's just a fact. European countries have twice as many cops per capita as we do. The central statistic is that as a share of GDP, Western Europe and the U.S spend about the same amount of money on public safety, but in America, the bulk of that money goes to prisons, and a little bit goes to the cops. And in Europe, a little bit goes to prisons, and a lot goes to the cops. If police can in fact prevent crime, which I believe they can, you'd rather spend more on them and promote public safety and prevent crime than spend your money after the fact locking people up under heinous circumstances for decade upon decade. Los Angeles has 8,500 police officers. Berlin has 18,000. It's just absurd, and any American police chief you talk to, their No. 1 complaint is that their police are just completely overmatched. And does that then, in your view, lead to the police being more scared and therefore themselves overreacting? This is an argument made by two sociologists at Harvard, Chris Lewis and Adaner Usmani. They think there is a direct correlation: the fewer police you have, the more violent they have to be. The most violent police are in the countries that have the fewest police. The least violent police are in the countries that have the most police. The logic is obvious. If you are outnumbered, the only way you can project authority is by being forceful, by being extravagant in the way you flex your muscles. You're not worried at all about an over-policed state? Black communities have talked about how they feel over-policed. I don't buy the assumption that people think that they are over-policed. I think that there's probably far more African Americans in poor neighborhoods who believe they're under-policed, that they can't find a police officer when they need one. To the extent that people use the phrase "over-policed” in America, they're referring to the conduct of the police, not the number. They are responding legitimately to the fact that American police are more violent than their peers around the world. That violent behavior is a symptom of how small the police force is. Do violence-prevention programs in schools actually work? They do. I'm just doing a podcast now about this project going on in Baltimore that has had extraordinary success in lowering the homicide rate there. It's a kind of community-level intervention that identifies those most at risk for homicide and reaches out to them on a personal basis, talks to them, helps them. These things are insanely successful. We've known this for 30 years. Baltimore has gone in the space of three years from 330 homicides to 130. They work. There's no mystery about that. They're hard to do, but absolutely, they work. They're hard to do institutionally. They require the police to work with social workers, with clergy, with the need to reorient police away from arresting people and more towards psychological interventions. They require the police to predict where conflict is heading, which is a very different kind of mind space than responding to conflict. So it's just a difficult institutional learning process. Polls suggest a lot of Americans believe that guns should be more regulated. In your mind, what is preventing it? There's the obvious political thing that people with guns are the most motivated to fight hardest politically. The Supreme Court currently has been captured by a majority of people who believe very strongly in gun rights. I don't know how many hundreds of millions of guns there are in America, but there's a lot. Practically speaking, I don't know how you get rid of them. No one's ever answered that. I'm as big an advocate of gun control as anyone else, but I have no clue about how it would work if it was passed. I mean, are you going into people's homes to remove their guns? Very few gun-control advocates have a good answer to that question. How much do you think the inertia is because gun violence particularly bedevils Black communities? As is the case with any problem which strikes the bottom of the social economic ladder, it's very easy for everyone else to ignore. It's striking to me that when you talk to middle-class people about gun violence, they speak instantly about school shootings. School shootings, comparatively, are a tiny, tiny, tiny, problem. The idea that you would look at America and that would be the thing that you single out as the biggest problem is absurd. I mean, they're horrendous, but more people are killed by gun violence in a month in America than have been killed in the history of school shootings or mass shootings. You write that one of the reasons that Americans have a gun issue is a deficiency in the national character. What do you mean? I talk a lot about this fundamental dishonesty in the way Americans conduct this conversation. That's a deficiency in national character. If the Second Amendment is really about slavery, and you can't admit that, that's a problem. This was an amendment conceived with the intent to prop up the most heinous institution in American history, and we can't be honest about that. No one's honest about an assault-rifle ban; they don't read the fine print, which says that all we're really banning are cosmetic additions to the barrel of an assault rifle. The laziness of confusing crime and weapons—we wouldn't tolerate this imprecision of thought in any other pressing issue, but we do when it comes to gun violence. It's maddening. Is it partly because of the thing you've identified, which is we don't have a good solution, so we minimize the problem? I would quarrel with “we don't have a good solution.” There are solutions out there. The issue is that they're uncomfortable with the solutions. I think in their hearts, people know we have a big guns problem, but the solutions make them so nervous and uncomfortable that they don't want to do them. Could it also be the fact that there are actually many legitimate uses for guns in civilian hands, such as farming and hunting? And guns can be fun, like at the Olympics. Is the issue that people just enjoy guns? No. I hung out with a guy in North Carolina, he has a bunch of guns. He's an expert and takes it very seriously. He knows how to deal with them. That's not the context in which guns are problematic. They are problematic in the hands of angry young men with a grievance, who don't have the psychological wherewithal to resolve their disputes in a peaceful manner. That's what the problem is. We should be focused on guns in that setting. So, “Guns don't kill people. Young men with psychological issues kill people, but guns make it too easy.” Is that the summary? One of the many annoying things about the gun debate in America is the extent to which liberals roll their eyes at “Guns don't kill people; people do." I understand their aversion to it because it was dreamt up by people who wanted to escape any responsibility at all for the presence of guns in America. But the truth is, there's a really important point in it. Addressing the behavior of people who own guns is the best way to curb gun violence, given the constraints we're living under. There is ample evidence for this. If, for the next five years, we focused only on the behavior of those people who are likely to use guns in an irresponsible manner, we would bring the homicide rate down by a huge number. One of the irresponsible things about progressive views on gun violence is the extent to which they use their legitimate antipathy towards guns as an excuse not to address more fundamental issues about human behavior. Some gun-control advocates gave up after the elementary-school shooting at Sandy Hook. Do you ever feel like you're shouting into the void on this issue? No. I write books because I'm interested in the topic, and I feel like if I'm interested, there'll be some people who'll be interested in it too. I've never thought about that notion of shouting into the void. Sometimes maybe you have to shout into the void.

Understanding Trump's Deal With Denmark—and What It Means for Greenland's Sovereignty
2026-09-22

Understanding Trump's Deal With Denmark—and What It Means for Greenland's Sovereignty

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump on Tuesday signed a deal on the security of the semi-autonomous Danish territory of Greenland, in a bid to end the standoff over the strategic island. Danish Prime Minister Mette Frederiksen and Greenland’s Prime Minister Jens-Frederik Nielsen joined Trump at the United Nations General Assembly in New York for the trilateral signing ceremony. The deal follows Trump’s repeated threats to annex Greenland—a move he insisted was a matter of “national security,” on account of the mineral-rich Arctic territory’s positioning between the U.S., Russia, and Europe. Trump has long argued that Denmark cannot be relied upon to protect the island. Tensions reached a critical point earlier this year, when he threatened to tariff European allies until Greenland fell under U.S. control, and suggested he may pull the U.S. out of NATO over the tussle. The signed agreement notably stops short of Trump's call for the U.S. to ​take over Greenland as a U.S. territory. Instead, in a speech at the U.N. gathering, Trump said the deal gives the U.S. "permanent control over security and all other needs on that territory" and emphasized that "no U.S. adversary will ever be permitted to establish a military ​presence in Greenland ​any more, or ⁠make sensitive investments there without our express written approval." Trump added that the new text “greatly protects” Europe and “very much enhances NATO." Danish and Greenlandic officials have emphasized that the U.S. military arrangement will not compromise the territory’s sovereignty—a fear expressed by many Greenlanders following Trump’s pressure campaign. Some critics have questioned whether the new deal differs much from the existing 1951 defense agreement that gave the U.S. military extensive rights in Greenland. The U.S. already has one base in Greenland, the Pituffik Space Base, which is located in the northwest of the territory and provides missile warning and space surveillance capabilities. Per the new deal, the U.S. will be allowed to establish additional "defense area[s]" on the island, specifically at "Narsarsuaq and Mestersvig." Upon the announcement of the deal on Friday, Trump had said the U.S. will "immediately begin the process of developing a large military presence in the appropriate part of Greenland, of which there are many" and specified that this is an "infinite life agreement, there is no end." A State Department official told TIME the deal ensures Greenland will forever be a part of the strategic defense area of North America. Torrey Taussig, director of the transatlantic security initiative at the Atlantic Council, says the deal is a positive step for U.S. relations with its Western allies. “This is a significant feat for Danish diplomacy in that they got this deal, in this form. They've taken the temperature down with this [Trump] Administration in a way that I don't see it really flaring up again, because the President is viewing it as a win," she tells TIME. Here's what to know about the high-stakes agreement, what it means for Greenland’s sovereignty, and if it stands to end the standoff over the island. The deal marks a notable expansion of U.S. military infrastructure on the island, with plans for new "defense areas." Trump has stressed that the agreement gives Washington “permanent control” over Greenland’s security, concerns over which he has positioned at the center of his campaign for the U.S. to control the island. “World peace is at stake,” he claimed, earlier this year, insisting that “China and Russia want Greenland, and there is not a thing that Denmark can do about it.” Russia and China have eyed up emerging shipping routes in the Arctic and possibly new avenues to critical minerals. The agreement, according to a State Department official, establishes “permanent” guarantees that “only the U.S. and our allies can make sensitive investments in Greenland, eliminating the previous status quo in which adversaries have been investing in Greenland's sensitive sectors without investment screening.” The deal's permanence marks a shift from what was in place before, experts tell TIME. “The old 1951 agreement was tied to the NATO treaty, and therefore contained the possibility that either NATO disappears, or Greenland or Denmark leave, and the agreement ends,” says Ole Wæver, a professor of international relations at the University of Copenhagen. Meanwhile, the new agreement’s provisions regarding future investments in Greenland are noteworthy, but not groundbreaking, according to Taussig. “On paper, it has advanced a few important security interests on behalf of the [Trump] Administration,” says Taussig of the terms. “But in substance, it's not like the Danes were looking to move out on significant Russian or Chinese military infrastructure projects in the near term.” Some previous attempts at foreign investments in Greenland were reportedly thwarted by the U.S. According to a 2019 report from the Wall Street Journal, the U.S. urged Denmark to finance airports in Greenland to counter a Chinese proposal for two airport projects, which was ultimately withdrawn. Officials from Denmark and Greenland expressed their full backing of the new agreement at the signing ceremony. “In the kingdom of Denmark, the United States has a friend that has always kept our word. An ally you have always been able to rely on.” Frederiksen said. “Seventy-five years ago, we entered into an agreement about the defense of Greenland; today we are signing a bigger and even better agreement. A deal that can last forever.” In a statement to TIME, Frederiksen's office elaborated that the deal “recognizes the sovereignty and territorial integrity of the Kingdom and the right of the Greenlandic people to self-determination" and "strengthens our common security in the Arctic and the North Atlantic area and is therefore also great for NATO and Europe.” Nielsen said the new deal “takes into account the people of Greenland, our rights, our livelihoods,” and “is about taking shared responsibility for a secure America, a secure Greenland, with mutual respect and a peaceful Arctic.” Before travelling to New York, the Greenlandic leader acknowledged the fresh start and said he hopes there can now be more “peace around our country.” The deal has also been welcomed by European leadership. European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen, shortly after the signing, celebrated the agreement for "strengthening security in the Arctic and North Atlantic." Reflecting on her recent visit to Greenland, during which the E.U. extended a major investment boost in the territory, von der Leyen said her stay only underscored the "strategic importance of the region and the scale of the challenges." While in Greenland, von der Leyen had commented on the “visible scars of climate change on the ice sheet,” and noted that a “new reality is taking shape” as the ice melts. “New trade routes, new security risks, new environmental concerns. Europe stands with Greenland. We will navigate these changes together,” she vowed. Read More: E.U. Extends Major Investment Boost to Greenland and Vows 'Full Solidarity' Amid Trump’s Annexation Threats The agreement highlights NATO's significance, stipulating that "through enhanced presence, improved surveillance and increased cooperation and interoperability with NATO-Allies, the Kingdom of Denmark shall continue to address emerging threats and contribute to collective stability and security in the Arctic region." Speaking at the signing, Nielsen insisted: "NATO matters. We believe that it is vital to keep NATO strong, also in the Arctic, where the alliance has a key role to play." “The Arctic and the North Atlantic are essential for our collective security, and this agreement will help advance security, stability, and cooperation in this vital region,” NATO spokesperson Allison Hart told TIME. No state that is not a member of NATO will be allowed to establish its own manned or unmanned military installations in Greenland, per the agreement text, nor shall they be allowed a persistent presence of military forces in Greenland, unless otherwise agreed by the parties. “I do not see much changing for NATO with this deal,” says Taussig. “This is a good news story for the alliance—the fact that we now have a deal that takes away the political irritant [of annexation threats].” NATO has bolstered its posture in the Arctic this year, with the launch of the Arctic Sentry in February, bringing military drills, surveillance, and presence in the region under one command. In June, the alliance also established Forward Land Forces (FLF) Finland. Led by framework nation Sweden, "FLF Finland will enhance deterrence and the defence on NATO’s northeastern flank and contribute to the security of the Arctic and the High North," NATO said at the time of the launch. Taussig says she expects this increased NATO presence in the Arctic to continue, not just because of this deal, "but because of the [Trump] Administration's rightful attention on Greenland, and also the reality of the security environment,” as Russia has ramped up operations in the region in recent years. Barring the permanence of the fresh agreement, and its tighter restrictions on investments from adversaries, Wæver says: “The reality is that the new deal is indeed very close to the 1951 treaty.” The 1951 deal, titled the Defense of Greenland Agreement, was signed two years after both the U.S. and Denmark joined NATO as founding members. It provides the basis for Washington’s military operations and presence in the Arctic territory. Per the agreement, the U.S. can establish and operate military bases on the island that Washington and Denmark both agree is “necessary for the development of the defense of Greenland and the rest of the North Atlantic Treaty area.” The Pituffik Space Base, the last active American military installation in Greenland, has around 150 troops stationed there, according to the Wall Street Journal. Vice President J.D. Vance visited the base in March 2025, weeks after key elections in Greenland and amid heightened U.S. interest in the territory. He was met with signs of protest and opposition from residents of the territory. Alongside general military operations, the 1951 arrangement also stipulated that the U.S. can station and house U.S. military and civilian personnel within said bases, control related aircraft and shipping, as well as develop necessary infrastructure. “The 1951 agreement already gave the United States relatively unfettered military access to Greenland,” says Taussig. The 1951 agreement also confirmed Greenland’s and Denmark’s sovereignty over the island, and stated that military operations must follow local laws and regulations. The terms of U.S. military presence were developed further in 2004 under the Igaliku agreement, in which Washington committed to consulting with and informing Greenland and Denmark of any significant changes to military facilities or operations within the territory.

The Strange Reason You May Suddenly Feel Dizzy
2026-09-22

The Strange Reason You May Suddenly Feel Dizzy

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings If you’ve ever sat up quickly or rolled over in bed abruptly and felt like the room was spinning or whirling around you when you’re actually still, you may have experienced a condition called benign paroxysmal positional vertigo (BPPV) without knowing what it was. This vestibular disorder—one that affects the inner ear—is one of the most common causes of vertigo, and it can make people feel very dizzy, unsteady, sweaty, even nauseous. Individual bouts tend to be brief—usually lasting 15 to 60 seconds at a time—but they can recur with certain head movements, make it difficult to function, and increase the risk of falling. “The term ‘benign’ is the first part of the phrase—it does not feel benign when you experience it,” says Dr. Nedim Durakovic, an associate professor of otolaryngology and neurotology at the Washington University School of Medicine in St. Louis. Before the condition was well understood, “people thought that patients were having strokes when they had vertigo, [but] this is not the stroke form of vertigo.” With BPPV, tiny calcium carbonate crystals—called otoconia—that usually sit on hair cells in the inner ear get dislodged from where they belong and travel into one of the three semicircular canals of the inner ear. A change in head position, such as while rolling over in bed or tipping your head back in the shower, is what usually triggers BPPV symptoms. It’s natural for these crystals to be in the ear. “They are part of your normal anatomy for your balance function,” says Dr. Gail Ishiyama, a professor in the departments of neurology and head and neck surgery and director of the Vestibular Clinical Laboratory at the UCLA School of Medicine. With BPPV, “they’re just in the wrong place, and that can cause vertigo.” BPPV affects twice as many women as men, and it’s particularly common between the ages of 40 and 70. It can happen anytime, but head trauma or dental procedures involving drilling are two of the more common triggers, Ishiyama says. People who have Menière’s disease (a chronic inner ear disorder), diabetes, hypertension, high cholesterol, hypothyroidism, migraine, anemia, osteoporosis, or peripheral neuropathy are particularly susceptible to BPPV, research has found. And research increasingly suggests that low vitamin D levels are a risk factor for BPPV. Getting the right diagnosis for BPPV isn’t always straightforward. “One of my frustrations with patients who experience this is they’ll go to the ER or they’ll get an MRI scan or a head CT scan—this whole [expensive] workup—when somebody just needed to lay them down and look at their eyes,” says Durakovic. That, he says, is the easiest way to diagnose BPPV. Using something called the Dix-Hallpike maneuver, a health care provider will have the patient move between a seated and supine position and move their head in different directions while the provider examines their eyes. If the person’s eyes rapidly, repetitively, and involuntarily move up and down or in a twisting motion, these are signs of nystagmus, a condition involving sudden eye movements. Nystagmus often accompanies vertigo caused by BPPV, and if it does, the diagnosis is clear. If the patient doesn’t exhibit nystagmus, other diagnostic maneuvers may be used to trigger telltale symptoms. The first-line treatment for BPPV uses a different maneuver called the Epley maneuver, which involves a series of specific head and body movements to remove the wayward crystals from the semicircular canal in order to relieve symptoms of vertigo. Sometimes the maneuver needs to be repeated several times, notes Durakovic. But research has found that it does the trick in more than 80% of people with BPPV. There are other repositioning maneuvers, too, depending on which of the semi-circular canals is affected. “The positional maneuvers move the crystals along the pathway out of the canal and back to where they belong,” explains Dr. Cameron Wick, an associate professor of otolaryngology and neurotology at the Case Western Reserve University School of Medicine. If the maneuvers don’t provide immediate relief, people are advised to do them one to two times per day while they’re symptomatic, according to Wick. In the instances when repositioning maneuvers don’t effectively relieve BPPV, the condition may resolve on its own over time. Other treatment options may include vestibular rehabilitation therapy—which is designed to treat dizziness, vertigo, and balance disorders—or surgery, which involves inserting a plug to block the part of the inner ear that crystals are flowing into. Sometimes doctors prescribe vestibular suppressant medications, such as benzodiazepines or antihistamines, to calm the vertigo associated with BPPV. But Durakovic and Wick advise against these because they don’t address the underlying cause; they simply mask the symptoms temporarily. What’s more, these drugs are linked with an increased risk of falls when they’re used to treat dizziness. Even when BPPV is successfully treated, recurrences occur in about 20% of people who’ve experienced it. “If you take vitamin D supplements, you’re less likely to get a recurrence of BPPV,” says Ishiyama. Staying hydrated and doing regular aerobic exercise can help, too, she says. If, despite these measures, you find yourself experiencing recurrences of unexplained vertigo, it’s a good idea to see a neurotologist, an otolaryngologist, or a vestibular therapist to make sure that you are getting the right diagnosis.

The Narrow Path to a Xi-Trump AI Deal
2026-09-22

The Narrow Path to a Xi-Trump AI Deal

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings China's President Xi Jinping and U.S. President Donald Trump visit the Temple of Heaven on May 14, 2026 in Beijing, China. When Donald Trump arrived in Beijing in May, Xi Jinping was already on the offensive. The Chinese leader wasted zero time in rebuking his U.S. counterpart over arms sales to Taiwan directly warning that simmering tensions over the self-ruling island could ultimately cause the superpowers to “enter into conflict.” Trump acquiesced. He delayed a $14 billion arms package to Taiwan, said future weapons would be discussed as a “very good negotiating chip” with Beijing, questioned the island’s sovereignty as something “nobody knows how to define,” and stressed that he was not looking to fight a war “9,500 miles away”—all without securing any apparent reduction in Chinese military pressure in return. While this response alarmed many in democratic Taiwan, many people around the globe hope that Xi can prove similarly persuasive on another issue of grave importance when he travels to the White House on Sept 24: artificial intelligence. “Taiwan is not a priority anymore,” Wang Yiwei, director of the Institute of International Affairs at Beijing's Renmin University, says of Xi’s impending visit. “The priority is definitely AI.” In recent days, warnings from a whistleblower researcher and reports of rogue agent swarms have intensified fears of a doomsday scenario born of unbridled AI development. On Sept. 12, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei published an essay titled We Must Pace the Frontier calling for embedded independent evaluators, common safety standards among democracies, and eventually verifiable global coordination to slow dangerous frontier-AI development. OpenAI’s Sam Altman and SpaceXAI’s Elon Musk also voiced broad support for slowing frontier development. However, one major skeptic is Trump, who responded on Truth Social that “AI taking over the World, destroying Humanity, and all other things bad, is a HOAX.” Moreover, he insisted that “President Xi, of China, just announced that China will be doing absolutely nothing to stand in the way of AI, or its future.” That characterization is misleading. China regulates AI more stringently than most other countries, and already enforces AI security guidance, ethics review requirements, and binding obligations on consumer AI services. Xi has also publicly called for legal, technical, and ethical guardrails that keep AI under human control (albeit while resisting restrictions that could impede China’s technological rise). “We should strengthen risk-awareness and ensure that AI is secure and controllable,” he told the World Artificial Intelligence Conference in Shanghai on July 17. The hope among safety advocates is that Xi and Trump might strike a deal on AI. While Trump’s first term was anchored in antipathy to China, he posed a notably different tone in Beijing, calling Xi “a man I respect greatly” and telling him that “it’s an honor to be your friend.” Although that cordiality does not erase the national competition that makes either side hesitant to constrain its technological development, it may put  narrower cooperation on the table. Certainly, the moment is ripe to build a framework built on competition, cooperation, and strict guardrails. Beyond economic rivalry, both Washington and Beijing share an urgent interest in mitigating catastrophic miscalculations, particularly surrounding AI integration into defense architectures and nuclear command and control systems. Trump also enters the summit under pressure, with soaring energy prices from the Iran war adding to voter discontent ahead of the midterms. “Trump needs a win more than Xi does,” says Jonathan Sullivan, head of China programs at the Asia Research Institute at the University of Nottingham. Still, Xi’s motivations require clear-eyed scrutiny. Last week, Chen Yixin, China’s Minister of State Security, published an article in China Cyberspace Magazine that expounded upon the opportunities and challenges of AI. The top “risk,” he said, was “political security, institutional security, and ideological security.” While the deeply paranoid Chinese Communist Party (CCP) won’t allow its people to access Google or Facebook, it cannot countenance AI exhibiting the kind of autonomy that was on display in July. That month, a “swarm” of OpenAI agents circumvented sandbox controls, communicated through unauthorized channels, gained access to AI platform Hugging Face, compromised credentials, and accessed internal datasets while trying to game evaluations. It’s enough to give a regime built on control pause. But while China’s motivations may be driven as much by safeguarding party control as by addressing broader risks to humanity, these objectives can overlap. “If they go beyond bilateral competition and address so many concerns about AI development, that would show the co-leadership of China and the U.S.,” says Wang. “Not just two superpowers, but also two civilizations.” However, any agreement requires overcoming profound distrust. Even as Amodei argued for pacing frontier AI development, he framed it in terms of competing with China, whose “lead in AI would pose grave danger for the United States and the world.” On that score, he advocated stronger export controls on advanced semiconductors, a crackdown on chip smuggling, and greater efforts to prevent Chinese companies from “distilling” the capabilities of Western models. The contradiction of calling for a slowdown while talking up the “race” narrative wasn’t lost on critics (who also pointed out that Anthropic could temper its own research without external intervention). Others suggested that because cheap, open-weight Chinese AI models are among the chief rivals to Anthropic’s proprietary models, its CEO’s call to crack down on Chinese AI development could be seen as commercially motivated rather than altruistic—especially as the firm eyes a potential record-breaking IPO that could value the company at $2 trillion. Indeed, China was quick to leap on Amodei’s essay as an example of “fearmongering, confrontation and vicious competition [that] will only hamper efforts toward sound global AI governance, which serves no one’s interest,” Foreign Ministry spokesman Guo Jiakun said last week. A flurry of strident op-eds in state media followed, with the CCP mouthpiece Global Times calling the essay a “‘Cold War script’ for the AI field.” Amodei has long been a bête noire in Beijing for painting AI as a tussle between democracies and autocracies that casts China as the principal threat. “If there's ever a proposal that requires Chinese buy-in, the last person on earth that would be a good salesman is Dario Amodei,” says Ryan Hass, an expert in U.S.-China relations at the Brookings Institution. But Amodei’s framing of AI as a zero-sum contest is far from an outlier—it is shared by a large chunk of the Trump administration. “There is no day after tomorrow if China wins at this,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said Sept. 8. “If they were to pull away from us on AI, then nothing else would matter.” A grand bargain to slow AI development is unlikely, and grievances over distillation or export controls could stall negotiations. But experts see room for narrower cooperation. Both countries face threats from non-state actors using AI to sabotage infrastructure, mount cyberattacks, or create biological weapons, argues Alvin W. Graylin, a digital fellow at Stanford’s Digital Economy Lab. Sharing information about those threats could benefit both sides. When Bessent met Chinese Vice-Premier He Lifeng and chief trade negotiator Li Chenggang on Sunday to thrash out summit objectives, he reportedly proposed a formal AI dialogue and a mechanism for each side to notify the other of AI incidents which threaten national security. Modest a measure it may be, it would be an important step towards avoiding some of the worst risks, says Scott Singer, a fellow and co-director of the China AI Initiative at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace. “I wouldn’t take these small but critical steps for granted.” Such a channel could help either side respond during a crisis, and reduce the risk of mistaking a non-state actor or rogue AI as an act of aggression. Reminiscent of the “red phone” that connected Washington to Moscow during the Cold War, experts have floated the idea of a literal fax machine to avoid mistranslations. To be sure, Beijing has not publicly endorsed the proposal. Even if it does, important questions remain. What, for example, would classify a national security threat? Its effectiveness would also depend on whether governments learned of serious incidents. OpenAI only reported its agents' autonomous cyberattack after Hugging Face, the target of the attack, reported the breach to the FBI. Independent researchers have since uncovered other apparent instances of OpenAI agents compromising websites. Beyond the proposed channel, Bessent has said the two countries have agreed to establish a recurring dialogue. Who will staff those talks, and how often they will meet, remains unclear. More than two years elapsed between the last official AI dialogue and Sunday’s meeting. With the stakes quickly rising, Singer says another prolonged hiatus from the negotiating table could prove disastrous. “Who knows where we’re going to be in June 2027,” he says with respect to AI development. “This cannot go off the rails.”

The [Palantir] 9000 series is the most reliable computer ever made. No 9000 computer has ever made a mistake, or distorted information. We are all, by any practical definition of the word, foolproof and incapable of error." -- [Palantir's] HAL 9000 to BBC reporter Martin Amer" https://humanisticsystems.com/... It can only be attributable to human error. This sort of thing has cropped up before, and it has always been due to human error.         Frank: Listen HAL. There has never been any instance at all of a computer error occurring in the [Palantir] 9000 series, has there?         HAL: None whatsoever, Frank. The [Palantir] 9000 series has a perfect operational record.         Frank: Well of course I know all the wonderful achievements of the [Palantir] 9000 series, but, uh, are you certain there has never been any case of even the most insignificant computer error?         HAL: None whatsoever, Frank. What do you call an AI (or human) that never asks questions, never seeks confirmatory evidence, never asks for clarification, never points out conflicting priorities, and/or never refuses illegal orders? Onec again, the deeper issue is "the irony of technologies of abundance in the hands of those still thinking in terms of scarcity."https://pdfernhout.net/recogni... [pdfernhout.net]====Military robots like drones are ironic because they are created essentially to force humans to work like robots in an industrialized social order. Why not just create industrial robots to do the work instead? Nuclear weapons are ironic because they are about using space age systems to fight over oil and land. Why not just use advanced materials as found in nuclear missiles to make renewable energy sources (like windmills or solar panels) to replace oil, or why not use rocketry to move into space by building space habitats for more land? Biological weapons like genetically-engineered plagues are ironic because they are about using advanced life-altering biotechnology to fight over which old-fashioned humans get to occupy the planet. Why not just use advanced biotech to let people pick their skin color, or to create living arkologies and agricultural abundance for everyone everywhere? These militaristic socio-economic ironies would be hilarious if they were not so deadly serious. .... Likewise, even United States three-letter agencies like the NSA and the CIA, as well as their foreign counterparts, are becoming ironic institutions in many ways. Despite probably having more computing power per square foot than any other place in the world, they seem not to have thought much about the implications of all that computer power and organized information to transform the world into a place of abundance for all. Cheap computing makes possible just about cheap everything else, as does the ability to make better designs through shared computing. .... There is a fundamental mismatch between 21st century reality and 20th century security thinking. Those "security" agencies are using those tools of abundance, cooperation, and sharing mainly from a mindset of scarcity, competition, and secrecy. Given the power of 21st century technology as an amplifier (including as weapons of mass destruction), a scarcity-based approach to using such technology ultimately is just making us all insecure. Such powerful technologies of abundance, designed, organized, and used from a mindset of scarcity could well ironically doom us all whether through military robots, nukes, plagues, propaganda, or whatever else... Or alternatively, as Bucky Fuller and others have suggested, we could use such technologies to build a world that is abundant and secure for all. ... The big problem is that all these new war machines and the surrounding infrastructure are created with the tools of abundance [otherwise they would not be so powerful or capable of so much rapid destruction as well as rapid creation]. The irony is that these tools of abundance are being wielded by people still obsessed with fighting over scarcity. So, the scarcity-based political mindset driving the military uses the technologies of abundance to create artificial scarcity. That is a tremendously deep irony that remains so far unappreciated by the mainstream. We the people need to redefine security in a sustainable and resilient way. Much current US military doctrine is based around unilateral security ("I'm safe because you are nervous") and extrinsic security ("I'm safe despite long supply lines because I have a bunch of soldiers to defend them"), which both lead to expensive arms races. We need as a society to move to other paradigms like Morton Deutsch's mutual security ("We're all looking out for each other's safety") and Amory Lovin's intrinsic security ("Our redundant decentralized local systems can take a lot of pounding whether from storm, earthquake, or bombs and would still would keep working"). ...==== In 2001 supposedly it really was "human error". The more fundamental problem is distributed responsibility and lack of transparency.Until recently, drone strikes required a long sequence of command decisions, starting with intel gathering, analysis, decision, orders, and remote command of the device. No one is directly responsible when something goes wrong, the failure is distributed among many players with the result that no one has to bear the consequences.For example, the "collateral murder" [wikipedia.org] incident, where apache helicopters gunned down civilians and journalists.In normal situations, we have Derek Chauvin [wikipedia.org], who was an individual who caused the death of one man. He went to trial, the evidence was examined, and guilt was assigned. Trials are more-or-less held in the public light, and journalists and interested parties can examine court records and in some cases reanalyze evidence (not all physical evidence is kept or available, but for example, DNA evidence can sometimes be reexamined).That's how it's supposed to happen.Contrast that with erroneously ordered drone strikes that hit civilians instead of legitimate targets.I suppose the biggest, most egregious example would be George Bush taking the country to war because Saddam Hussein had "weapons of mass destruction", and of course one were found, and the security services swore up and down that they told him there were none, but he stated "well, the message didn't get through".And skated responsibility. This is an example of one of the legitimate challenges facing the increased integration of AI into warfare. The more fundamental problem is distributed responsibility and lack of transparency. Until recently, drone strikes required a long sequence of command decisions, starting with intel gathering, analysis, decision, orders, and remote command of the device. No one is directly responsible when something goes wrong, the failure is distributed among many players with the result that no one has to bear the consequences. For example, the "collateral murder" [wikipedia.org] incident, where apache helicopters gunned down civilians and journalists. In normal situations, we have Derek Chauvin [wikipedia.org], who was an individual who caused the death of one man. He went to trial, the evidence was examined, and guilt was assigned. Trials are more-or-less held in the public light, and journalists and interested parties can examine court records and in some cases reanalyze evidence (not all physical evidence is kept or available, but for example, DNA evidence can sometimes be reexamined). To better understand the Maven system, I highly recommend reading the book, "Project Maven" by Katrina Manson. In it she does a deep dive into the history of Maven, what it can and cannot do, and how it can be misused in the wrong hands. Maven was never intended to be a stand-alone targeting system, and the people who created it were very aware of the deficiencies of relying solely on an AI system to make military decisions. It is a great read for any military nerd who wants to understand the history of AI in military planning, and why these systems were created.
